========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess the main thing is that I’m just losing a lot of time to checking. Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.

And it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.

It’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.

Like with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.

At work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.

So… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.

I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. There’s definitely a part of me that knows it’s excessive. Like, if I step back from it, I can say, “You cleaned the wound area. You reviewed the CAD file. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.

But in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.

So I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. It’s almost like knowing it doesn’t make sense doesn’t stop it from feeling urgent.

It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.\n\nBut in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.\n\nSo I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They feel unwanted, definitely. I mean, I do worry about things normally — my job kind of requires being careful — but this has a different quality to it. It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.

Like, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.

Same with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.

And the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.

Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.\n\nSo I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. It’s almost like knowing it doesn’t make sense doesn’t stop it from feeling urgent.\n\nDoctor: When these checking thoughts show up, do they feel like unwanted intrusions that don’t really fit with how you want to think, or do they feel more like your normal way of worrying about things, just stronger than usual?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted, definitely. I mean, I do worry about things normally — my job kind of requires being careful — but this has a different quality to it. It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.\n\nLike, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.\n\nSame with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\n[Last Question]\nOn a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.

On a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.

And it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.

Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.\n\nIt’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.\n\n[Last Question]\nApart from this repeated checking of things, do you have any other recurring, unwanted thoughts or rituals—like needing to wash in a particular way, arrange things symmetrically, repeat certain actions, or mentally go over phrases or numbers—that you feel driven to do to relieve anxiety?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly it’s the checking. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.

It’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version. I’ll do the check, and then instead of feeling done, I get this feeling like, no, you need to go through it again, because if you move on now you’re being careless. So I’ll repeat the same routine, sometimes in almost the same order, until the anxiety drops a little bit.

I wouldn’t say it’s mainly about symmetry or numbers or phrases, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I need to prove to myself that I didn’t miss something. But the proof doesn’t really stick, so I keep going back. And it can be really hard to stop because it feels like I’m not “cleared” to continue until I’ve checked enough times.

Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.\n\nIt’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. There’s definitely a part of me that knows it’s excessive. Like, if I step back from it, I can say, “You cleaned the wound area. You reviewed the CAD file. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.\n\nBut in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.\n\nLike, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.\n\nSame with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.\n\nDoctor: Apart from this repeated checking of things, do you have any other recurring, unwanted thoughts or rituals—like needing to wash in a particular way, arrange things symmetrically, repeat certain actions, or mentally go over phrases or numbers—that you feel driven to do to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the checking. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version. I’ll do the check, and then instead of feeling done, I get this feeling like, no, you need to go through it again, because if you move on now you’re being careless. So I’ll repeat the same routine, sometimes in almost the same order, until the anxiety drops a little bit.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s mainly about symmetry or numbers or phrases, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I need to prove to myself that I didn’t miss something. But the proof doesn’t really stick, so I keep going back. And it can be really hard to stop because it feels like I’m not “cleared” to continue until I’ve checked enough times.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past few weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, even when you’re not actively dealing with the checking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I get frustrated and worn down by all of this, and sometimes after a long stretch of checking I feel pretty low or kind of defeated, like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” But when I’m not actively stuck in that loop, I don’t know that I’m walking around feeling sad all day.

It’s more like stress and exhaustion, I guess. Or irritation with myself. There are moments where I feel okay, especially if I’m distracted or doing something that isn’t triggering the checking. So… no, I don’t think it’s been this constant depressed mood separate from the checking. At least not that I’ve noticed.

It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. So… no, I don’t think it’s been this constant depressed mood separate from the checking. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of these checking situations, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about lots of other everyday things, like finances, health, family, or work in general, even when there’s nothing specific to trigger it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in that broad, constant way. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone does — if there’s an actual deadline at work, or if something expensive comes up, or if there’s a real reason to be concerned. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just sitting around all day jumping from finances to family to health to every possible thing that could go wrong.

The anxiety feels much more tied to specific situations where I think I might have missed something or done something wrong. Like if there’s a concrete thing to verify, that’s when I get stuck. Outside of that, I can usually think about other parts of life without spiraling too much. I’m not saying I’m perfectly relaxed, because I’m obviously not, but it doesn’t feel like this generalized worry about everything for no reason. It’s more… triggered by the checking-type situations.

Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.\n\nIt’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. There’s definitely a part of me that knows it’s excessive. Like, if I step back from it, I can say, “You cleaned the wound area. You reviewed the CAD file. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.\n\nBut in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.\n\nSo I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. It’s almost like knowing it doesn’t make sense doesn’t stop it from feeling urgent.\n\nDoctor: When these checking thoughts show up, do they feel like unwanted intrusions that don’t really fit with how you want to think, or do they feel more like your normal way of worrying about things, just stronger than usual?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted, definitely. I mean, I do worry about things normally — my job kind of requires being careful — but this has a different quality to it. It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.\n\nLike, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone does — if there’s an actual deadline at work, or if something expensive comes up, or if there’s a real reason to be concerned. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just sitting around all day jumping from finances to family to health to every possible thing that could go wrong.\n\nThe anxiety feels much more tied to specific situations where I think I might have missed something or done something wrong. Like if there’s a concrete thing to verify, that’s when I get stuck. Outside of that, I can usually think about other parts of life without spiraling too much. I’m not saying I’m perfectly relaxed, because I’m obviously not, but it doesn’t feel like this generalized worry about everything for no reason. It’s more… triggered by the checking-type situations.\n\n[Last Question]\nBecause of this checking and rechecking, have you actually been missing deadlines, getting in trouble at work, or having problems in your relationships or daily life that wouldn’t be happening otherwise?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… unfortunately, yes. That’s part of why I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep telling myself it was a “bad habit” or me being extra careful.

At work, my project deadlines have started slipping. Not because I don’t understand the work or because I’m not doing it — it’s more that I get to the point where something should be ready to submit, and then I just can’t feel comfortable sending it. I’ll stay late going back through the same files, same dimensions, same notes, like I’m trying to get to this point where I feel certain enough. And sometimes that “certain enough” point never really comes, or it comes way later than it should. So something that should take, I don’t know, ten minutes to review turns into an hour or two, and then the whole schedule shifts.

I haven’t had some huge blow-up or anything, but there have definitely been delays that I don’t think would be happening if I could just check once and move on. And I feel embarrassed because from the outside it probably just looks like I’m slow or disorganized, when in my head I’m trying really hard not to mess anything up.

It’s affecting my life outside work too. I used to be better about going to paddleboarding meetups or getting workouts in, and now I miss those a lot because the checking routines eat up the time I used to keep for myself. I’ll think I’m going to leave at a certain time, and then I get stuck rechecking something before I can go, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained to bother. So yeah, it’s not just annoying anymore — it’s actually pushing things out of my life.

Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.\n\nIt’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. There’s definitely a part of me that knows it’s excessive. Like, if I step back from it, I can say, “You cleaned the wound area. You reviewed the CAD file. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.\n\nBut in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.\n\nSo I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. It’s almost like knowing it doesn’t make sense doesn’t stop it from feeling urgent.\n\nDoctor: When these checking thoughts show up, do they feel like unwanted intrusions that don’t really fit with how you want to think, or do they feel more like your normal way of worrying about things, just stronger than usual?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted, definitely. I mean, I do worry about things normally — my job kind of requires being careful — but this has a different quality to it. It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.\n\nLike, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.\n\nSame with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.\n\nDoctor: Apart from this repeated checking of things, do you have any other recurring, unwanted thoughts or rituals—like needing to wash in a particular way, arrange things symmetrically, repeat certain actions, or mentally go over phrases or numbers—that you feel driven to do to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the checking. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version. I’ll do the check, and then instead of feeling done, I get this feeling like, no, you need to go through it again, because if you move on now you’re being careless. So I’ll repeat the same routine, sometimes in almost the same order, until the anxiety drops a little bit.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s mainly about symmetry or numbers or phrases, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I need to prove to myself that I didn’t miss something. But the proof doesn’t really stick, so I keep going back. And it can be really hard to stop because it feels like I’m not “cleared” to continue until I’ve checked enough times.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, even when you’re not actively dealing with the checking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. Or irritation with myself. There are moments where I feel okay, especially if I’m distracted or doing something that isn’t triggering the checking. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these checking situations, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about lots of other everyday things, like finances, health, family, or work in general, even when there’s nothing specific to trigger it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that broad, constant way. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.\n\nSame with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.\n\nDoctor: Apart from this repeated checking of things, do you have any other recurring, unwanted thoughts or rituals—like needing to wash in a particular way, arrange things symmetrically, repeat certain actions, or mentally go over phrases or numbers—that you feel driven to do to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the checking. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version. I’ll do the check, and then instead of feeling done, I get this feeling like, no, you need to go through it again, because if you move on now you’re being careless. So I’ll repeat the same routine, sometimes in almost the same order, until the anxiety drops a little bit.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s mainly about symmetry or numbers or phrases, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I need to prove to myself that I didn’t miss something. But the proof doesn’t really stick, so I keep going back. And it can be really hard to stop because it feels like I’m not “cleared” to continue until I’ve checked enough times.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, even when you’re not actively dealing with the checking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I get frustrated and worn down by all of this, and sometimes after a long stretch of checking I feel pretty low or kind of defeated, like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” But when I’m not actively stuck in that loop, I don’t know that I’m walking around feeling sad all day.\n\nIt’s more like stress and exhaustion, I guess. Or irritation with myself. There are moments where I feel okay, especially if I’m distracted or doing something that isn’t triggering the checking. So… no, I don’t think it’s been this constant depressed mood separate from the checking. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these checking situations, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about lots of other everyday things, like finances, health, family, or work in general, even when there’s nothing specific to trigger it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that broad, constant way. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone does — if there’s an actual deadline at work, or if something expensive comes up, or if there’s a real reason to be concerned. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just sitting around all day jumping from finances to family to health to every possible thing that could go wrong.\n\nThe anxiety feels much more tied to specific situations where I think I might have missed something or done something wrong. Like if there’s a concrete thing to verify, that’s when I get stuck. Outside of that, I can usually think about other parts of life without spiraling too much. I’m not saying I’m perfectly relaxed, because I’m obviously not, but it doesn’t feel like this generalized worry about everything for no reason. It’s more… triggered by the checking-type situations.\n\nDoctor: Because of this checking and rechecking, have you actually been missing deadlines, getting in trouble at work, or having problems in your relationships or daily life that wouldn’t be happening otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. That’s part of why I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep telling myself it was a “bad habit” or me being extra careful.\n\nAt work, my project deadlines have started slipping. Not because I don’t understand the work or because I’m not doing it — it’s more that I get to the point where something should be ready to submit, and then I just can’t feel comfortable sending it. I’ll stay late going back through the same files, same dimensions, same notes, like I’m trying to get to this point where I feel certain enough. And sometimes that “certain enough” point never really comes, or it comes way later than it should. So something that should take, I don’t know, ten minutes to review turns into an hour or two, and then the whole schedule shifts.\n\nI haven’t had some huge blow-up or anything, but there have definitely been delays that I don’t think would be happening if I could just check once and move on. And I feel embarrassed because from the outside it probably just looks like I’m slow or disorganized, when in my head I’m trying really hard not to mess anything up.\n\nIt’s affecting my life outside work too. I used to be better about going to paddleboarding meetups or getting workouts in, and now I miss those a lot because the checking routines eat up the time I used to keep for myself. I’ll think I’m going to leave at a certain time, and then I get stuck rechecking something before I can go, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained to bother. So yeah, it’s not just annoying anymore — it’s actually pushing things out of my life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… I’ve probably always cared about doing things correctly, especially with engineering stuff. Rechecking, really. It’s not just one thing, either — it’s like my dressing supplies, the door locks, file dimensions at work, revision notes… I keep going back over them.\n\nAnd it’s not like I genuinely find something wrong every time. It’s more that I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times. Like if I stop too early, the anxiety is still sitting there, and I can’t focus on the next thing. So I do it again, and sometimes again, until it drops enough that I can leave it alone.\n\nIt’s starting to eat up hours, and I’m an engineer, so checking details is part of my life anyway, but this feels different. It feels less like being careful and more like being stuck.\n\nDoctor: When you feel you have to recheck things, is it usually because you’re getting intrusive, unwanted thoughts or mental images about something bad happening if you don’t, or is it more just a general sense of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nDoctor: When these doubts and ‘what if’ thoughts pop up, do you feel, at least part of you, that they’re excessive or don’t really make sense, or do they seem completely reasonable to you in the moment?\n\nPatient: I’d say… both, but not equally, if that makes sense. There’s definitely a part of me that knows it’s excessive. Like, if I step back from it, I can say, “You cleaned the wound area. You reviewed the CAD file. You’re not being rational by going back again and again.” I can see that.\n\nBut in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to dismiss. It’s like the thought has this weight to it. Even after I know I’ve cleaned properly, or I know I looked at the dimension, the thought cuts in again — what if I missed one tiny thing? What if that one thing is the thing that matters? And then my brain jumps straight to, well, then the wound gets infected, or the design has some flaw, or someone gets hurt because I was careless.\n\nSo I don’t fully believe it in a calm, logical way, but I also can’t just reason it away. That’s the frustrating part. I’ll try to argue with it, like, “No, I already checked,” but it doesn’t really resolve. It’s almost like knowing it doesn’t make sense doesn’t stop it from feeling urgent.\n\nDoctor: When these checking thoughts show up, do they feel like unwanted intrusions that don’t really fit with how you want to think, or do they feel more like your normal way of worrying about things, just stronger than usual?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted, definitely. I mean, I do worry about things normally — my job kind of requires being careful — but this has a different quality to it. It feels like the thought barges in after I’ve already done the reasonable thing.\n\nLike, I’ll have cleaned the wound area, and I know I cleaned it. I can remember the steps. But then this thought cuts across everything like, what if you missed one tiny bit, what if that tiny bit is enough, what if it gets infected because you were careless? And it doesn’t feel like me thoughtfully assessing risk at that point. It feels more like I’m being interrupted by doubt.\n\nSame with a CAD file. I can review it and know I looked at the dimension, but then the thought comes back that maybe I misread one number, or missed one small thing, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that could hurt someone. And I don’t want to be thinking that way. I’m not sitting there choosing to imagine worst-case outcomes. It just kind of forces itself in.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I can tell myself, “You already checked, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t land. The thought keeps feeling urgent anyway, like I can’t just reason it away or move on like I should.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, roughly how much total time do you spend doing these checking routines before you can move on to other things?\n\nPatient: It adds up to hours, honestly. That’s part of why I’m here, because it’s not just one quick check and then I’m done.\n\nOn a typical day, if I’m working and also dealing with my dressing supplies at home, it can easily be a couple of hours total, sometimes more, spread out in pieces. Like I’ll check the dressing supplies, then wash or arrange things, then go back because I’m not sure I did it “right” enough. With work files it’s the same kind of thing — dimensions, revision notes, whether I saved the right version — I can lose big chunks of time rereading or reopening things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I get some satisfying feeling that everything is definitely okay. It’s more like I have to repeat the routine enough times that the anxiety drops just a bit, enough that I can force myself to continue. But then later it can start again with door locks or another file or something else. So, yeah… I’d say hours in total, not minutes.\n\nDoctor: Apart from this repeated checking of things, do you have any other recurring, unwanted thoughts or rituals—like needing to wash in a particular way, arrange things symmetrically, repeat certain actions, or mentally go over phrases or numbers—that you feel driven to do to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the checking. I mean, that’s really what brings me in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things before I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nIt’s the dressing supplies, the door locks, work files… dimensions in CAD, revision notes, whether I saved or looked at the right version. I’ll do the check, and then instead of feeling done, I get this feeling like, no, you need to go through it again, because if you move on now you’re being careless. So I’ll repeat the same routine, sometimes in almost the same order, until the anxiety drops a little bit.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s mainly about symmetry or numbers or phrases, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more like I need to prove to myself that I didn’t miss something. But the proof doesn’t really stick, so I keep going back. And it can be really hard to stop because it feels like I’m not “cleared” to continue until I’ve checked enough times.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, even when you’re not actively dealing with the checking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I get frustrated and worn down by all of this, and sometimes after a long stretch of checking I feel pretty low or kind of defeated, like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” But when I’m not actively stuck in that loop, I don’t know that I’m walking around feeling sad all day.\n\nIt’s more like stress and exhaustion, I guess. Or irritation with myself. There are moments where I feel okay, especially if I’m distracted or doing something that isn’t triggering the checking. So… no, I don’t think it’s been this constant depressed mood separate from the checking. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these checking situations, do you also find yourself worrying excessively about lots of other everyday things, like finances, health, family, or work in general, even when there’s nothing specific to trigger it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that broad, constant way. I mean, I worry about normal things like anyone does — if there’s an actual deadline at work, or if something expensive comes up, or if there’s a real reason to be concerned. But it doesn’t feel like I’m just sitting around all day jumping from finances to family to health to every possible thing that could go wrong.\n\nThe anxiety feels much more tied to specific situations where I think I might have missed something or done something wrong. Like if there’s a concrete thing to verify, that’s when I get stuck. Outside of that, I can usually think about other parts of life without spiraling too much. I’m not saying I’m perfectly relaxed, because I’m obviously not, but it doesn’t feel like this generalized worry about everything for no reason. It’s more… triggered by the checking-type situations.\n\nDoctor: Because of this checking and rechecking, have you actually been missing deadlines, getting in trouble at work, or having problems in your relationships or daily life that wouldn’t be happening otherwise?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. That’s part of why I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep telling myself it was a “bad habit” or me being extra careful.\n\nAt work, my project deadlines have started slipping. Not because I don’t understand the work or because I’m not doing it — it’s more that I get to the point where something should be ready to submit, and then I just can’t feel comfortable sending it. I’ll stay late going back through the same files, same dimensions, same notes, like I’m trying to get to this point where I feel certain enough. And sometimes that “certain enough” point never really comes, or it comes way later than it should. So something that should take, I don’t know, ten minutes to review turns into an hour or two, and then the whole schedule shifts.\n\nI haven’t had some huge blow-up or anything, but there have definitely been delays that I don’t think would be happening if I could just check once and move on. And I feel embarrassed because from the outside it probably just looks like I’m slow or disorganized, when in my head I’m trying really hard not to mess anything up.\n\nIt’s affecting my life outside work too. I used to be better about going to paddleboarding meetups or getting workouts in, and now I miss those a lot because the checking routines eat up the time I used to keep for myself. I’ll think I’m going to leave at a certain time, and then I get stuck rechecking something before I can go, and by the time I’m done it’s too late or I’m too drained to bother. So yeah, it’s not just annoying anymore — it’s actually pushing things out of my life.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to before these checking problems became this intense, would you say you’ve always been a very perfectionistic, rigid, rule‑bound person in most areas of life, or did this way of needing things to be just right really start only with the checking getting worse recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate those, honestly. I mean… I’ve probably always cared about doing things correctly, especially with engineering stuff. Like I don’t like sloppy work, and I’ve always been someone who wants the answer to be right, not just “close enough.” But I don’t know that I’d describe my whole personality as rigid or rule-bound in every area of life.\n\nBefore the checking got worse, I could still usually finish things and move on. I might double-check something important, or spend extra time making sure a calculation made sense, but it didn’t feel like this whole internal battle where I couldn’t stop. And outside of work, I don’t think I was someone who needed every little thing in my life to be perfect. I could be flexible with plans, I could leave for something without getting trapped in the same way.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I had some perfectionistic tendencies, maybe, but not like this. This needing things to feel absolutely just right or certain seems like it became a much bigger problem when the checking started getting more intense. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking, but it doesn’t feel like I’ve always been this way across the board.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s more than just a vague unease, I think. There is definitely a thought that cuts in, and it feels very specific even if I know, logically, that I already checked.\n\nLike with the wound dressing stuff, even after I’ve cleaned the area and I can remember doing it, this thought comes in like, what if you missed one tiny spot, or what if your hand brushed something and you didn’t notice, and then it turns into an infection because you were careless. And I’ll try to tell myself, no, I cleaned it properly, I followed the steps, but it doesn’t really stick. It’s like the doubt just resets.\n\nAt work it’s similar with CAD files or dimensions. I’ll review a file and know I looked at the measurement, but then I get this thought that maybe I misread one number or missed one constraint, and somehow that turns into a design mistake that hurts someone later. It sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I can’t just move on, because if something did happen I’d feel like I’d ignored a warning.\n\nSo… yes, there’s unease, but it’s usually attached to that kind of thought — that I missed one tiny thing and it could have serious consequences.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
